                                              11.12
Obviously, this function is finite and satisfies

                                        Isub (m2 ) = 0.                                (11.58)

The corrected two-point function then takes the simplified form
                                               1
                     K(p) =                                       + ... .              (11.59)
                               p2 + m2 − m2 κ2 Isub (−p2 ) −ı̊ϵ

We can even go one step further and decide to add a counterterm for the kinetic
term
               Las,int = − 21 ζ(∂ϕ)2 − 21 µ′ m2 ϕ2 − 16 κmϕ3 − 24
                                                               1
                                                                  λκ2 ϕ4 . (11.60)

This term changes the overall normalisation of the field ϕ(x) away from its
canonical choice, but allows us to normalise the residue of the asymptotic particle
pole in the two-point function to 1 at all orders. In this case, we can drop the field
strength renormalisation factor Z by setting it to 1.
In conclusion, the asymptotic Hamiltonian describes the canonically normalised
fields with appropriate physical masses. The interaction Hamiltonian contains all
types of allowable interactions terms. Their parameters are tuned to stabilise the
masses and normalisations and to match with physical interaction processes.


Power Counting. Can we understand under which circumstances a QFT model
is renormalisable? We can use a crude argument in terms of the mass dimensions
of interaction terms.
Consider a Feynman diagram in momentum space describing a single loop without
further sub-trees or external propagators attached to it. It evaluates to an integral
over a rational function of the momenta and masses of the particles

                                        dℓD Q(ℓ)
                                     ∫︂
                                  I∼             ,                            (11.61)
                                           P (ℓ)

where the denominator P (ℓ) originates from Feynman propagators and the
numerator Q(ℓ) from vertices and spinor traces.
Earlier, we have discussed that UV divergences of the integral must be polynomials
of the external momenta pk . Furthermore, we can argue that particle masses in the
denominator P can be safely ignored for the purpose of UV divergences because
they are always dominated by the momenta.17 Contributions from the masses mk
to divergences therefore originate from Q alone (or equivalent parts of the
integral), and will only appear as polynomials in the final answer. Evidently, the
coupling constants αk will appear as overall factors.
  17
    A notable exception is the propagator of a massive vector field which has unaccompanied
factors of mass in the denominator. Such factors upset the power counting scheme, and in order
to preserve it, masses of vector field should be generated by the Higgs mechanism.




                                            11.13
Altogether this implies that the structure of UV divergences of a loop integral is
given by polynomials in the momenta, masses and coupling constants with
numerical coefficients
                                   dI
                         P :=           ∈ Poly(αk , pk , mk ).               (11.62)
                                d log Λ

Next, let us consider the mass dimensions of the above quantities: On the one
hand, the mass dimension of the integral is determined by the mass dimensions γk
of the fields on the external lines of the Feynman diagram. One finds
                                             ∑︂
                                   [I] = D −     γk .                      (11.63)
                                                  k

On the other hand, the variables pk and mk of the divergence polynomial P have
mass dimension 1 by construction. We furthermore make the assumption that all
terms in the Lagrangian Lk have a mass dimension bounded from above by the
spacetime dimension D or, equivalently, that all the coupling constants αk have a
non-negative mass dimension
                              [Lk ] ≤ D,        [αk ] ≥ 0.                   (11.64)
The assumption thus implies that a UV divergence may only occur if [I] = [P ] ≥ 0.
Altogether, divergences can appear only for terms with 0 ≤ [P ] ≤ D. There are
only finitely many independent polynomials in the pk that fit within these bounds.
In particular, the configuration of external legs and their momenta pk , which
correspond to derivatives ∂k , determines the corresponding counterterm Lk , so
only finitely many counterterms are needed to cancel all potential divergences.
Note that there is a crucial difference between coupling constants with strictly
positive mass dimension and dimensionless coupling constants. As the dimension
of coupling constants accumulates, dimensionful coupling constants, [αk ] > 0,
contribute divergences only at low perturbative orders. When there are also
dimensionless coupling constants present, [αk ] = 0, arbitrary loop orders will be
divergent. Conversely, if some of the coupling constants have a negative mass
dimension [αk ] < 0, this can lead to divergence polynomials of arbitrarily large
negative mass dimension which require a steadily increasing number of
counterterms at higher perturbative orders. Such models are called
non-renormalisable, for example, the quantisation of general relativity is of this
kind.
In our example, we have included all interaction terms of mass dimension bounded
from above by 4. For each divergence which can possibly arise, there is a
corresponding counterterm.
When considering Feynman diagrams which contain a divergent loop along with
some other external tree structures, it can be shown that the loop can be replaced
by a universal counterterm corresponding to the loop to cancel the divergence. A
subtle issue at higher loops are overlapping loops, where it may not be evident
which counterterms to use. Fortunately, it can be shown that this situation does
not leave behind divergences which cannot be accounted for.

                                        11.14
11.5          Vertex Renormalisation
Let us briefly discuss how to renormalise the remaining divergences at the one-loop
level in our example.


Divergent Interactions. By power counting arguments we can derive that
divergences can appear in our model only for two-sided loops.18 Loops with three
or more sides are perfectly finite. There are only three potentially divergent terms
in our model.


                                                                                 (11.65)


One of them we have already discussed, the other two integrals can be made finite
by adding appropriate counterterms of the form ϕ3 and ϕ4 to the Lagrangian

                       Lint = − 16 κmϕ3 − 241
                                              λκ2 ϕ4 + Lct ,
                       Lct = − 21 µ′ m2 ϕ2 − 16 κ′ κmϕ3 − 24
                                                          1 ′ 2 4
                                                             λκ ϕ
                                                                                 (11.66)
                              =             +              +                 .


Suitable counterterm coefficients to make all observables finite at one loop read

            µ′ = κ2 I(m2 ),       κ′ = 3κ2 λI(m2 ),       λ′ = 3κ2 λ2 I(m2 ).    (11.67)

As discussed above, µ′ is determined by a stable physical mass at m. There is no
similar universal condition for the coefficients κ′ and λ′ ; any finite shift with
respect to the above values is permissible, it merely leads to a reparametrisation of
our model.
We have already seen that the ϕ2 counterterm effectively replaces the loop integral
by a finite subtracted loop integral

                                     I(−p2 ) → Isub (−p2 ).                      (11.68)

Exactly the same replacement is achieved by our above choice of counterterms for
ϕ3 and ϕ4 .

                                                                                 (11.69)

Quantum Electrodynamics. Ultimately, we would like to sketch the
application of counterterms to quantum electrodynamics. Using the fact that the
mass dimensions of the spinor field ψ and the vector potential A are 3/2 and 1,
respectively, there are 5 types of potentially divergent correlators in D = 4
spacetime dimensions:
electron self-energy ⟨ψ̄ψ⟩: [. . .] = 3

                                                                            (11.73)

contributes to the field strength renormalisation Ze and mass me of the electron;
vertex renormalisation ⟨ψ̄Aψ⟩: [. . .] = 4


                                                                            (11.74)


contributes to the effective electron charge q;
vacuum polarisation ⟨AA⟩: [. . .] = 2

                                                                            (11.75)

contributes to the photon field strength renormalisation Zγ only; mass shift for
photon is not permitted by gauge symmetry (corresponding counterterm AA not
permitted either);

                                                    11.17
photon self-interactions ⟨AAA⟩, ⟨AAAA⟩: [. . .] = 3, 4


                                                                           (11.76)


diagrams are finite due to gauge symmetry (corresponding counterterms A3 and
A4 not permitted either).
Altogether, all divergences in (one-loop) Feynman diagrams can be compensated
by renormalisation of the couplings Ze,γ , me and q or by introducing counterterms
for these couplings.
